ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e319	The autonomy of patients and human rights are important and fundamental principles of all treatment. In the field of mental health protection, volunteering in treatment and the reduction and proper use of coercive measures are a goal for the whole service. But we have to admit that in any case, in some situations and towards some patients, one will have to use coercion, including security and control measures, for the benefit of the patient himself and/or for the sake of other people and society. It would be naive not to take seriously that there is a small group of patients with severe mental illness who at the same time have an increased risk of serious violence. These are considered to be particularly dangerous for other persons. This group is not homogeneous, and the danger is not necessarily related to the mental disorder. The danger may be linked to other aspects of an individual, demographic or contextual nature. The draft law that the Stortinget deals with in this matter deals with the security provisions of regional security departments, the legal basis for setting up and security provisions around an entity with a particularly high level of security and the establishment of a national coordination unit. The legislative changes do not include conditions of coercive treatment, transfer to regional security departments or the scope/length of treatment, even if the debate around the law could give the impression for a period of time. The case includes significant and significant changes of major importance to patients, employees and relatives associated with the Regional Security Departments and covered by the National Coordination Unit. There are issues that affect human rights, individual autonomy and the principle relationship between punishment and treatment. In the proposition, the government has thoroughly addressed these issues, both related to human rights, the Basic Law and legal decisions affecting these issues. The patient base of the regional security departments indicates that violence is a current issue in these departments. Violence may affect other patients, visitors and personnel and persons outside the departments. Different recording procedures have shown that there are a number of events that can be linked to this. At the Health Bergen HF Regional Security Section, 67 episodes of serious injury to personnel were recorded in 2011. That same year at St. Olav's Hospital, 494 claims were recorded caused by a total of 14 patients. In the consultation round of the legislative proposal, improved security in relation to these departments has received broad support from those responsible for the units, the professional organisations concerned and the patient organisations. Although there is disagreement in the Committee's position on a single point, I feel that there is broad political support for the need for better security provisions in regional security departments, the need for the establishment of specific security provisions in an entity with a particularly high level of security and the establishment of a national coordination unit. Today’s law requires justified suspicion of examination of the patient’s person, space and assets. Body cavity examination is not permitted, except under emergency law. These limitations on the feasibility of the study mean that the professional community considers that there is uncertainty about the level of risk for co-patients, personnel and the environment. The requirement of concrete suspicion and the prohibition of patient cavity investigations can either lead to an excessive level of safety, if the law requires practice, or the practice becomes such that the legal basis and legal certainty of the practice are not sufficient. Therefore, the establishment of better security procedures relating to regional security departments and the possibility of more routine control of entry and exit is fully supported within the Committee. The same applies to the creation of a separate unit with a particularly high level of safety. The transfer criteria are very strict. This will be an offer that may occasionally be needed for a few extremely few patients, and the main difference between this and an ordinary department will be that the security measures that can be used in a regional department will be mainly mandatory in such a department. There is also broad support for the establishment of a national coordination unit, which will provide a greater opportunity to have an overview of patients sentenced to transfer to mental health protection.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e342	I would like to start by thanking the rapporteur for a good report and for a good committee reading. I am pleased that there is a broad majority in the Committee on the proposal submitted. The legislative proposal covers the security provisions of the Regional Security Departments, the legal basis for establishment and the security provisions of the entity with a particularly high level of security and the establishment of a national coordination unit. Like the rapporteur, I would like to point out that the changes to the law do not therefore include conditions of coercion, transfer to regional security departments or the scope/length of treatment, although many in the media have focused on such issues. The patient base of the regional security departments indicates that violent behaviour is a topical issue in these departments. Violence may affect other patients, visitors and personnel and, in the case of escape, persons outside the departments. Different procedures for recording aggression, threats, precursors of violence and violence show that these departments regularly do so. In 2011, 67 incidents of serious injury to staff were recorded at the Health Bergen Regional Security Section. In the same year at St. Olav's hospital, a total of 494 claims were recorded caused by a total of 14 patients. The Health South-East Regional Security Department has reported a number of cases, such as repeated threats, violence, hostage-taking, drug abuse and other criminal offences, which provide a basis for notification. So it's time to get better security from these departments. Several have pointed out the danger that the new rules to investigate assets, etc., looking for weapons and other things, will be anti-treatment. In addition, today’s situation is a treatment-retardant. Looking at the extent of situations in the regional security departments, there is every reason to believe that creating a safer framework for everyone there will work positively, including in relation to treatment. This was also pointed out by the Medical Association during the hearing. In rare cases, a unit with a particularly high level of safety may be required. Such a unit shall be part of a regional security department but may be physically located elsewhere. I am also pleased that an overall committee agrees to this, and that it can also be added, for example. prison. A prerequisite for this is that the unit meets the same requirements as health care institutions, both professionally and physically. The transfer to the unit shall be rare and only in exceptional cases. A transfer may only be adopted in specific cases where there is a particular risk of escape, hostage-taking, serious and violent behaviour or attack against the patient himself, co-patients or personnel. It is quite clear from the bill that these criteria underlie such a transfer. I believe that in such a safety assessment it would not be right to separate patients who have been sentenced to mandatory mental health protection from those who are not. It is not the basis for the centralisation of detention; the important thing is the risk of very serious incidents in society and the safety of patients and personnel. Patients who are not sentenced to mandatory mental health protection may also represent a particular safety risk. There are many examples of people who have been sentenced to ordinary prison sentences for different conditions, which can become psychotic during acquittal. They will need treatment in the field of mental health protection, and the necessary safety measures must also be available for such patients. But it is not possible to follow the proposals of the People's Party and the Christian People's Party for a vote. That the Progress Party believes this, is probably breaking with most of the things they have said in the past about such cases, and I wonder if it's just a glitch, or if they're actually resetting the course for their criminal welfare policy. If the last vote, it was anyway a strange place to start for the party that's trying to be tough on crime. Finally, I hope that today's debate will help to clarify some of the misunderstandings that have arisen in the media about the matter, and that you will be given a good idea of what the matter is actually about.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e363	In this case, it has been important to have a quick clarification, primarily for the sake of the legal proceedings in Oslo District Court. Therefore, this case has been dealt with with with an abnormally short consultation period before it was promoted, and at an unnaturally high pace in the committee – this in order to get the legislation in place as soon as possible. Several have responded to this approach, a vision of the Progress Party far on the road parts. Today’s legislation has been concerned with weaknesses, shortcomings and uncertainties in dealing with some issues both in relation to criminal law and in the field of mental health protection. It is therefore high time to carry out a thorough review of these issues, and it is therefore a pity that the matter was raised with such urgency. The Progress Party has more concerns about this matter. Let me first begin by stating that we believe that we must have a law that ensures that people who commit criminal acts must be able to stay away from society, even in their lifetime. There is no disagreement as to the need for such a tightening. Nevertheless, the Progress Party is concerned about the assessments that have been made and whether this amendment is contrary to the Constitution. We are concerned for two main reasons: firstly, we believe that the Storting should be careful to adopt new laws that may be contrary to the spirit of the Constitution, and secondly, that we have seen several issues in recent years where the government has made changes to the law that have subsequently been set aside, because they have been contrary to the Basic Law's provisions on retroactive effect. Despite these appeals, the Progress Party has nevertheless chosen to join the Ministry’s assessments, but with some critical comments. In this case, the Government also proposes a national coordination unit, the need for better interaction between the health and justice sectors and knowledge and governance data in order to better plan the provision of services. This supports the Progress Party. In one area, however, the Progress Party separates the layer by the majority, and it is on the question of who should be able to enter the new unit with a particularly high level of security, which was also addressed to the representative of Breen. The Progress Party considers it appropriate to move persons convicted of criminal offences and for forced mental health protection to such a unit. Then such a unit can be located within the prison walls for our part. However, we have major problems in accepting that even mentally ill people who have never committed any criminal acts should now be able to be put into prison in principle, along with those who have committed serious criminal acts. In our view, the proposal does not work properly. The legal certainty of the individual is almost violated, and the sickest of them are faced with criminals – contrary to what this group needs. The knowledge and attitudes of society towards the mentally ill are already very stigmatizing. Serious mentally ill people, who have never committed anything criminal, are today picked up by the police over health professionals, among others. due to lack of psychiatry ambulances. So now they have to be able to go to prison. What has been crucial for the Progress Party in this matter is the fact that the vast majority of those currently being treated in the regional security departments have never done anything criminal, but are now subject to the new law and thus equivalent to heavy criminals. The party believes that when the government opens up this, the whole focus of mental health protection is changed. In our opinion, there is a clear breach of what has been the long lines in the treatment of mentally ill people, namely a worthy treatment offer. The Health and Welfare Committee is committed to tightening up the law for people convicted of compulsory mental health protection, but the Progress Party will not introduce patients into these departments other than those who have actually done something wrong. In this way, we believe that we can both meet the needs of society for protection, while at the same time safeguarding the fundamental rights of sick people. I refer to the proposal on which the Progress Party is involved and which is referred to in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e650	At the request of the Energy and Environment Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 40 minutes and allocated by up to 5 minutes to each party and up to 5 minutes to a member of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e659	The main objectives of Norwegian petroleum policy are to facilitate the profitable production of oil and gas in a long-term perspective. This is achieved through increased field extraction, the development of findings and the detection of undiscovered resources. In order to ensure continued value creation on the continental shelf from a multi-generation perspective, an active policy must be put in place to prevent production declines and to provide opportunities for making new findings. Over the last 40 years, the petroleum industry has contributed significantly to the financing and development of the Norwegian welfare state. The issue we are dealing with today concerns the plan for the development and operation of the Martin Linge field. It's about reserves that are estimated at about 6 million standard cubic meters of oil, 20.4 billion standard cubic meters of gas and 2.9 million standard cubic meters of condensate. In total, this is equivalent to approximately 189 million barrels of oil equivalent. The operator’s economic analysis shows that the Martin Linge field has an expected present value before tax of SEK 7.6 billion in 2011. The planned expansion solution is based on the demand for power in the operational phase to be met by power from land via cable and the maximum consumption is estimated to be around 40 MW. The planned connection point is Kolsnes, and the concession request has been submitted and processed by NVE separately. The power situation in the area at start-up will make it appropriate to attach conditions to such a concession, precisely in order to ensure that the power system in the region is taken into account. In this context, it is an overall committee which stresses the importance of this not being an obstacle to shore-side power supply. An impact assessment has been carried out for the Martin Linge field without any evidence to suggest that the project should not be carried out or that remedial action should be implemented in addition to those underlying the development plans. Based on the operator's calculations, estimates made by the Oil Directorate, and assessments made by the Petroleum Authority, the development of the Martin Linge field appears to be a socially viable and sufficiently robust project, which can be carried out within acceptable safety, work environment, the external environment and fisheries interests. In addition to tax receipts from production in the field, development and operation will contribute to employment nationally and regionally. The total annual works expected to be created during the deployment phase and the exploitation phase are 16 100 and 12 700, respectively, including consumption effects. It is an overall committee that recommends the approval of the Martin Linge field development and operation plan.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e866	The matter we are dealing with now concerns plans for the development and operation of the Edvard Grieg field – also a great name, cf. previous case – formerly known as Luno. The rightholders are Lundin, Wintershall Norway and RWE Dea, where Lundin has half of the rights. The field was identified in 2007 and is planned to be developed with a bottom-solid device with wells, process facilities, auxiliary systems and residential quarters. An export solution for oil has been proposed via Grane Oil Pipe to Sture Terminal. The gas export solution will be via Sleipner or SAGE. The extractable reserves in the field are estimated to be 29.1 million standard cubic metres of oil equivalent. It equals 183 million barrels of oil equivalent. About 90 pst. of the extractable resources are oil, while the remainder is gas. It is planned to start production in the fourth quarter of 2015, and it is expected to produce over a period of 20 years. The operator's economic analysis says that the expansion has an expected present value before tax of 33.3 billion. 2012-krona. It will therefore make a significant contribution to the Norwegian community. Studies have found that there are significant co-ordination gains and cost savings in a coordinated development solution for the Edvard Grieg field and neighbouring Draupne. The rights holders of the two extraction permits have therefore entered into a coordinated development agreement, which facilitates the final processing and export of the production of a future Draupne device through the device in the Edvard Grieg field. A relatively large investment has already been made in the development of the Edvard Grieg field – before the Stortinget has taken its decision. Early in May, it was known that Lundin, as an operator, had entered into a contract with Kværner for the production of the deck for the platform. The contract value is assumed to be around 8 billion. kr. The Ministry of Oil and Energy has the possibility under the Petroleum Act to allow such early investments under special conditions. That's what's been done in this specific case. It is in isolation very happy to see that a mission of this size goes to a Norwegian supplier. At the same time, as rapporteur on this matter, I feel the need to stress that the handling by the Storting of plans for development and operation is sovereign. Early investments are made entirely at the risk of the builders themselves. It is important to identify – also in view of the treatment of future development plans. When large investments are initially to be expected after the Storting has taken its decision, it is because no binding and undue economic pressure should be placed on the Stortinge in terms of its ability to deal with matters on a free basis. It is important for me to stress that this is a principle that must not be challenged over time. So I'm not going to make any attempt to pull back from the previous case to the general debate on electrification, but just to state that the current policy is that force from land should be explored in connection with all the new fields. In the case of the Edvard Grieg field, the operator has found that the price per tonne of CO2 reduction for power from land is around SEK 700 if coordinated with the Draupne and Dagny. The cost of the Edvard Grieg field alone is intended to exceed NOK 1500. The joker who can seriously reduce costs, if you go for a coordinated solution in the area, is, of course, the Johan Sverdrup field. Now all operators at the southern part of the Utzira Height are required to determine an overall solution for power from land, which is expected to be completed by the end of the year. The plan for the expansion and operation of the Edvard Grieg field makes it clear that if there is an overall solution for power from land at the southern Utsira height, the Edvard Grieg field shall be associated with such a solution if the Ministry of Oil and Energy does not decide otherwise. In that case, the Edvard Grieg field shall cover its legitimate share of the investment and operating costs of the solution. It is therefore pointed out by a Joint Committee that the Edvard Grieg Facility must also be built in such a way that it is prepared for reception so that it can receive power from land if it becomes an appropriate solution. In the context of the climate notification, which is dealt with in this room later today, Stortinget sets an expressed target for electrification of the southern part of the Utzira Height. With these comments, it is a pleasure to say that there is an overall committee that recommends that the plan for the development and operation of the Edvard Grieg field be approved by the Stortinget.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e1086	In the future, climate change forecasts suggest that weather conditions in Norway will become wetter and warmer, and that the incidence of extreme weather in different variants is likely to increase. This has consequences in terms of the danger of flooding and migration in Norway, how we will live with this risk picture, and how we will meet this challenge in the future. The announcement of floods and migrations that we now have to deal with is therefore a daily and important matter with significance for both small communities and larger cities. State hazard monitoring has revealed that more than 150 000 people live in areas exposed to flooding or migration in Norway. In addition, there are a number of schools, hospitals, kindergartens, commercial buildings and the type of facilities. The attitude of the Energy and Environment Committee to the Stortinget shows that there is a broad consensus on the understanding of the danger potential for floods and the future and the organisation of responsibilities and work in the field of flood and pace problems. We have a lot of beautiful and exciting nature with high mountains and deep valleys, waterfalls, rivers and fjords. In Norway, we live pretty close to nature, and we are happy to use it — both by exploiting the resource base in the context of nutrition and for pleasure, recreation and experience. The message states that it is not possible to secure 100 pst. against any natural disaster. With Norwegian terrain and ground conditions, it is not realistic to avoid completely building in flood or sloping terrain, nor is it physically feasible or economically viable. However, we have to work within some main axes. Firstly, it is important to manage land use through good land planning. In this area, it is essential that there are clear responsibilities between the different levels of government: state, county and municipalities, and of course builders and private actors, and that land planning is based on knowledge. The notification indicates that the area still unexplained in terms of responsibility is who is responsible for the damage to existing buildings and the Committee agrees that a clean-up on this issue is necessary. The government announces such a review in the message. I understand that, for example. The municipality of Bergen had some challenges after the landslide at the Hatlestad terrace, where the municipality was almost sent from the department to the department, and where there were uncertainties about responsibilities. Of course, this is demanding for a municipality to deal with. In the wake of this incident, the municipality of Bergen carried out its own ROS analysis, a risk and vulnerability analysis, of the entire municipality. This one was expensive, but the municipality cost it itself. From the Right, we believe that the State needs to take greater national responsibility for monitoring, focus on knowledge development and research and provide training courses that provide relevant competences in these disciplines. People should be able to feel confident that areas such as spent for residential purposes shall be relatively safe in view of the danger of flooding and slippage. Rehabilitation and childbirth are common after a break in life, and in the case of a grant assessment — that is to say, the person paying child contributions when assessing his ability to pay — it is taken for example. consideration of the contributors, i.e. this person who pays contributions, new children, so as to ensure that they too are dependant. If the starting point is that the individual parent with the new partner is the closest to carrying the economic consequences of new children, I believe this must also apply to these children. There has to be symmetry and reciprocity here. Such a result would in that case mean that, in the context of contributions, there would in reality be no possibility of taking care of new children in any context, and there would be more rigid legislation. It may be desirable, but in that case I would like to be thoroughly evaluated. It is difficult to avoid the mutual impact of mother’s and father’s dispositions on the distribution of the child’s costs as long as they have a common responsibility for the care. However, the fact that parents have freedom of action equal to a 20 p.m. reduction in income signals that the parents must to some extent accept the other’s dispositions when they are no longer part of the same household. This may help to increase the tolerance between parents of individual choice and thus also to mitigate conflict. But it is clear, it can also be a source of conflict. In the light of the vote and the debate here today, I would also like to initiate a review of this 20 pst. rule, which was therefore not considered – let me stress it. The following was a rule that was changed from 1 January 2009 – it was an old case in which the Stortinget had agreed that we should change the legislation. What we are discussing is this 20 p.m. rule – which gives some flexibility and freedom of choice to parents. At least I can assure the representative Horne that the Council of State is perfectly capable of assessing the policy in this regard. The point is that this is not easy and that it is about taking care of children both in the household of the contributor and in the household of the contributor. My starting point is at least – and I will insist – that any change to the legislation must apply to both parties. It would be grossly unreasonable whether the beneficiary should be the only person who cannot make changes or cannot take into account any new children.

There will not be so many examples around the community that private individuals make large profits, until you can start questioning whether this is money that benefits your children. That is the background to today's proposal for regulation, that is to ensure legitimacy around all the public crowns that are now going to ensure good quality children's gardens. It's true that someone has been talking about here, that we have had one proposal at the hearing, where there were some relevant counter-conceptions. That is why we have now put forward a new proposal for Stortinget, which has a different logic in its structure. In this proposal, we do not go according to what is dividends or not dividends, but we promise that government grants and parental payments will benefit children in kindergarten. It is a very important principle that is now very clear in the law. We have other areas within the Ministry of Knowledge that have the same kind of principle. At the same time as the legislative proposal presented regulates the use of government grants and parental payments in non-municipal kindergartens, it ensures that municipalities have access to financial penalties where the municipal grant and parental payment are not used in accordance with the intention and purpose set out in the Children's Hospital Act. The bill ensures that the kindergarten sector will continue to have serious actors who want to use public funds and parental payments for the operation of high-quality kindergartens. In the context of the new legal provision, profits may be retained, provided that the accounts of the non-municipal kindergartens show that the funds have been used in accordance with the purpose. In the design of new legislation, the following principles have been central: The appropriations for kindergartens shall be granted to the children. They should not go to private property construction. The expectations of kindergarten owners must be clear through clear rules. New rules must ensure the legitimacy of the sector over time. We must give a clear signal to all actors that private kindergartens in Norway are too serious players who want to run good kindergartens. In order to ensure a clear regulatory framework, the same accounting reporting rules are important for all kindergartens. The legislation must apply to all non-municipal kindergartens, although there are very many different types of company. What we are now doing in concrete terms is: to introduce a purpose paragraph to require accounting and reporting requirements in line with the Accounting Act to require documentation of internal transactions to be subject to audit clearance. As far as the sum of these proposals is concerned, I am very pleased that the hearing bodies have accepted them so well, that there is no one dramatizing what kind of extra work this is going to be for kindergartens, and that KS and a lot of municipalities say that this is a good code that they can practice. I believe that these are rules that will ensure the digestion of the private kindergartens in the future, and that will prevent us from getting many examples of large profits, which could undermine the emergence of a predominantly serious industry.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e2163	The Ministry of Justice is adding to Prop. 95 L for 2011-2012 submit proposals for amendments to the Foreign Affairs Act. The Ministry proposes to amend Section 84 of the Foreign Affairs Act to allow the foreign authorities to obtain information about applicants or others covered by the Act directly from public bodies. The Ministry further proposes in a new section 84 A that the foreign authorities can process and reuse personal data, including sensitive personal data. Where a government agency has information about someone’s personal circumstances, the starting point is that this information is tacit, unless otherwise specified or clearly provided for by law. However, the proposal refers to the fact that the exchange of such information between public bodies can contribute to more rational treatment and thus reduce the length of proceedings. Shorter processing time is a gain both for society in general and for the individual user in particular. It should be noted in the case that the proposal at the same time involves better service for the user who does not submit the same information several times, or has to himself obtain information from one public body, in order to pass this information on to another. The proposal will also provide better assurance that the management uses correct information. The Ministry notes that in some situations it may be in the interests of the user to withhold information or to provide incorrect information to the non-resident administration in order to obtain goods or to avoid penalties. It is also pointed out that control is in favour of the autonomy of the foreign administration to obtain such information directly from another public body. As regards the objections raised by the Redd Barna, Jussbuss and MiRA Centre that the proposal should not be implemented because it involves major privacy interference and violates privacy and legal certainty, the Ministry considers that the benefits of the proposal outweigh those disadvantages, which the Committee unanimously supports. The European Convention on Human Rights Article 8 provides for protection against disclosure to unauthorised persons. In order for intervention in that right to be justified, certain conditions must be fulfilled. Firstly, it is necessary to decide whether the exchange of current information would at all involve an intervention in the individual's right to privacy, and if the exchange of information involves such an intervention, the intervention must be justified. It is pointed out in the proposition that, in line with the proposal, obtaining information would involve varying degrees of interference with the individual’s right to privacy. To the extent that the procurement involves a private intervention, the question of whether the conditions laid down in Article 8 of the ECHR are fulfilled. Firstly, the intervention must be justified by the objectives set out in that provision. The information collection is justified by the desire for the most effective regulation of who has resided in the territory of the country and by ensuring that this is done on the basis of correct information. Regulation of who has resided in the territory of the State is a fundamental and generally accepted right of the State. A fundamental objective of the foreign legislation is to ensure national security, public security and the economic well-being of the country and to prevent disorder or crime. It is pointed out in the proposition that the proposed information collection thus meets this condition. Data obtained for the treatment of a foreign matter will always have a dual purpose: it will both confer an advantage on the data subject through a residence permit and it will verify that he will not pose a social hazard. The proposition refers to the fact that the same information is later used in a case to assess flight safety in case of return. The need for information will therefore be covered by the original purpose of treatment, namely to control societal hazards. The re-use is in a close and natural context with UDI’s operations, in the sense that the assessment of aviation safety is usually carried out as part of the implementation of a decision to refuse an application for a residence permit, as UDI has previously taken. Furthermore, the re-use of information does not require greater data quality than its original use, as it would generally be more invasive to be refused a residence permit in Norway than to be refused to return without police support on the aircraft. On this basis, the Ministry considers that the re-use will not be incompatible with the original purpose of collection, cf. personal data law. Finally, I would like to point out that the Committee has assessed the content of Prop. 95 L for 2011-2012 and is unanimous in agreeing to amendments to the Law of 15 May 2008 No 35 on the access of foreigners to the kingdom and their residence here.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e2231	The climate challenge, together with hunger and poverty, is one of the greatest challenges facing the world. They're hanging out. If the world's poor are to be lifted out of hunger and poverty, large amounts of energy are to be needed, energy that today's technology will result in even greater greenhouse gas emissions. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change believes it is necessary to cut global greenhouse gas emissions by 50-85 pst. by 2050. But only in the next 20 years will the world's energy consumption increase by 50 pst. Countries outside the OECD will provide more than 90 pst. of that increase. Still, over a billion people in the world will live without electricity in 20 years. Today there are 1.4 billion people – more than 20 pst. by earth’s population — living without access to electricity. 2.7 billion people depend on traditional wood and other biomass combustion to make food. World Health Organization figures show that 1.6 million people die every year as a result of lung and breathing problems caused by cooking over open fire, mostly women and children. More than the deaths are caused by malaria, tuberculosis and HIV/AIDS. The availability of electricity is therefore the main prerequisite for raising the world's poor to a level of prosperity that is approaching us. At the same time, we need technological quantum leaps to realise the low-carbon society by 2050, and the rich part of the world needs to show developing countries that it is possible to combine high living standards with low emissions. The fundamental challenge in climate policy is market failure, or the fact that it is too cheap to pollute and too expensive to be environmentally friendly compared to the likely costs climate change will impose on us. The climate challenge is global. Therefore, this issue is best addressed by using a global pricing mechanism, such as: an allowance market in which all countries undertake. Unfortunately, there seems to be a long way to go before we achieve this, and it remains to be seen whether the promise of the UN Climate Change Conference in Durban to a global agreement from 2020 is actually being implemented. In the meantime, Norway needs to use other instruments that contribute to national emission reductions without increasing global emissions. Norway is currently linked to the European ETS market and, from 2013, approximately half of Norwegian emissions will be subject to a ETS obligation. This is an effective tool for reducing emissions in the sectors involved. For those sectors which are not subject to a quota obligation and are therefore not subject to competition, we are freer to implement any specialnormous measures without risk of carbon leakage. Norway is well placed to contribute to the fight against global climate change. We have technology, competence and capital to make a difference. The Technology Development Fund is one of the main instruments in the government’s climate communication. The decision of the majority suggests that the fund should be fully financed as early as 2016. The Fund shall contribute to the development and implementation of new and immature technologies. This will contribute to both research and development, which can in the long term make a major difference both at home and abroad. Norwegian industry is the only sector in Norway that has contributed significant emissions reductions over the past 20 years. Hopefully, the Technology Development Fund will enable them to do even more while enhancing competitiveness. It is an overall committee that underlines the importance of the Norwegian industry remaining in Norway. Norwegian industry is the world leader in environmentally friendly production. No one has been served to introduce framework conditions for industry to move from environmentally friendly hydropower to gas or coal on other continents. It is therefore essential that we contribute to making industry even more competitive and environmentally friendly. The Technology Development Fund will be able to contribute to this. If we are to realise the low-carbon society, it is essential that we succeed in developing a cost-effective technology for the capture and storage of CO2. This underlines the importance of the work of the Technology Centre in Mongstad, but also the importance of doing more in this area. It is therefore an overall committee that points out that Gassnova should explore the possibility of a demonstration project for solutions for the capture and storage of CO2 from industry and that meets Gassnova’s assessment of the possibility of completing a demonstration project for the capture and storage of a new coal-fired power plant in Longyearbyen. The Committee considers Norway to have the ambition to realise at least one full-scale CO2 capture and storage facility by 2020. In the construction sector, there is a high potential for reducing greenhouse gas emissions through simple grips. The public sector should lead by example. The majority therefore believe that fossil fuel oil as a base load must be phased out of all state buildings by 2018. The majority also agree to ban fossil oil firing in households and other buildings in 2020. This presupposes the introduction of sound support schemes for conversion to bio-oil, heat pump or other environmentally friendly alternatives as from 2013. However, the most environmentally friendly energy is that which is not being used. Therefore, it is paradoxical that Norway is the only country in Europe without an energy efficiency improvement target. The majority therefore ask the government to come back with a target for energy efficiency improvement in construction during 2012 and to propose to the Stortinget with instruments to trigger significant energy efficiency and energy conversion, from fossil to environmentally friendly sources in private households. In addition to the reinforced building technical requirements for newly built government alerts in the climate communication, climate policy in the building sector has become much more ambitious than the climate communication originally foreseen. It is important to note that very many energy efficiency measures have a short depreciation period and significantly reduce operating costs for the owner of the building. For individuals, the threshold is often slightly higher than in the industrial market. That's why good incentives are important. Transport emissions amounted to 32 pst in 2010. of Norway’s total greenhouse gas emissions. At the same time, this is a sector that is not exposed to carbon leakage. It is therefore in this sector that the potential for domestic cuts is greatest. In order to reduce emissions from this sector, it is necessary to phase in new and environmentally friendly vehicle technologies and to facilitate the choice of public transport, corridors and bicycles. The majority agree to strengthen the climate communication on a number of points in the transport sector. The majority determine, inter alia: strengthening the public transport reward scheme by 250 million. SEK in 2013 and 250 million. kr in 2014 and that documented results shall be prioritised. This will be an important contribution and incentive for big cities to invest heavily in public transport in the future. It will contribute to a significant reduction in the growth of emissions in the metropolitan area. It is also important to bet on the railway, not least in the commuting areas of the Central East. The majority therefore ask the government to submit proposals for rapid progress in the development of the intercity link in the Central East. The majority also requests the inclusion of a national public transport strategy as part of the National Transport Plan 2014-2023. In the field of transport, it is an important signal that the current tax advantages for the purchase and use of zero-emission cars will continue beyond the next charging period, provided that the number of such vehicles does not exceed 50000. All the batches in Stortinget are behind this proposal, which provides long-term and predictability for both buyers, users and sellers of such vehicles. In addition, the majority assume that fuel taxes and car taxes will contribute to making it more profitable and predictable to choose environmentally and climate-friendly in the years to come. Developments in the transport sector are largely determined in the national transport plan and in the state budgets in the future. Although the majority has achieved significant progress compared to the government’s original notification, much will depend on how the ambitious objectives and ambitions of the transport area are followed up in practice in the coming years. Let me end by mentioning some other areas covered by the message and setting. In the agricultural sector, the majority agree that a national cross-sectoral biogas strategy is needed. An overall committee agrees that Norway must contribute to strengthening the knowledge base of climate work. The Committee proposes inter alia: to increase the allocation of the research centres for environmentally friendly energy and to establish a new geothermal energy centre. The Committee also stresses the importance of the Swedish-Norwegian market for electricity certificates, but asks the government to evaluate the competitive situation between Swedish and Norwegian operators. The Committee review has helped to make the climate notification more ambitious and committed in important areas. The Plural setting is the result of an agreement between six of the seven parts of Stortinget. I am sorry that the government parties did not want to have conversations with all the parties on Stortinget. The opposition parties negotiated as a block, and there was therefore no reason to keep one of the lots out of further talks, not least because the negotiating position on which the settlement was based was actually developed by all four opposition parties in common. Then all the parties should be allowed to negotiate about them as well. Today, however, there is first and foremost reason to rejoice at the outcome, which is a better and more concrete climate policy. I thank the Committee on Cooperation in a demanding process and recommend the suspension of the majority.

What fascinates me when we travel around and talk to European sister parties and other political sister parties around the world is how important this agenda is to everyone. Sick for those with major economic challenges, this is one of the main themes around the coffee table in the evening, when discussing what it is important to work on in the future. I am therefore sorry that we have lost so many years working to get this message out. I am sorry that we have lost so many opportunities to take action. For Norway is in a small bubble with other countries in terms of having an economic space to transform our business into the new challenges we face. We have all these possibilities, and we could use these possibilities even more offensively – we could have done it years ago – to make the changes that we know we need to go through. We must not throw away the fantastic economic opportunity that Norway has today – while everyone else is struggling – to transform our business into meeting the challenges of the future, so to speak, when we know that Europe is going to be asking for new technological solutions, new products and new energy solutions in the future. So to a little bit of the content of the equation. Here it is cut in oil firing in private property and industrial buildings in 2020, as well as phased out oil firing in public buildings from 2018. It is important to have good support mechanisms, so that this is not socially distorted, but one is clearly on the right track. The second is to get a big, offensive effort on public transport on the road and on the road. I'm glad we're now getting a fairer reward scheme, where you reward on the basis of results, not on the basis of what the voters have voted in each city. This has been introduced in the United Kingdom and is now being investigated in a number of other countries. So it's important to say that the climate agreement is a minimum settlement. We're free to suggest even better action on it, and it'll probably be the Left to continue to do so. We might have wanted a different device for the climate technology fund, although we are very pleased that we got a faster escalation in the fund than the government has proposed. We will also fight for that in the future, in order to achieve better environmental solutions in industry, in private households and in the traditional industries, which are not the best in the climate. The ambition to build at least one new full-scale demonstration facility for capture and CO2 storage by 2020, I believe, is also an ambitious objective, which some of us believe we should be able to achieve. We are pleased that a separate biogas strategy is now to be developed. What I'm going to end with is saying that we assume that we disagree with some of the main reasons for the climate challenges. We in the left would have wanted this deal to be even wider, if it had been possible. We never got that opportunity to test, because it was more important for someone to keep out than to get it to as wide as possible. Then I have to say that we can always discuss who is Galileo Galilei, who fought for new knowledge against old tradition, and who is the pope, who fought for old tradition and old-fashioned thinking. I think that might not bring us any further. I had hoped that we would reach a broad agreement on measures here. It would have been good for democracy, and in such an important case as climate, the political game had also been put outside. But the left has kept the principle we've had from the beginning; that the important thing for us has been that as much agreement as possible that can bring us in a climate-friendly direction. For us it was important to have a reconciliation that we could put our name under, which we could actually stand for, sail if it wasn't the primary position of the left. We're glad we got to this deal, we're sorry that it didn't get wider. We're also sorry that we didn't get any more, but we stress here in the debate that this is a minimum settlement, and we want to fight to get some maximum solutions to the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e2693	This morning, Norwegian Elbil Association marked its joy over the climate agreement outside the Stortinget. Like environmental organisations, the elbileire are very pleased with the outcome of the climate communication and negotiations, and in particular with the guarantee we actually guarantee the electric car by continuing the current tax advantage until 2017 – a charging policy that has already led Norway to the top as the world's leading electric car. However, this is only one of many clear grips the batches have gathered around in the agreement to cut greenhouse gas emissions from the transport sector. It's necessary. In 2010, greenhouse gas emissions from the transport sector amounted to 32 pst. of Norway’s total greenhouse gas emissions. In cities, the sector accounts for more than half of the emissions. Passenger car traffic represents the largest share. Therefore, if we are to succeed in achieving the ambitious objectives of the agreement to cut domestic climate emissions, strong action must be taken on the whole sector. The Norwegian car park needs to be phased over to become cleaner and more environmentally friendly. Over the course of a few years, the government has contributed to a 30 pst drop in CO2 emissions from new cars in Norway. through changes in the charging scheme. The climate agreement underlines the need to continue this conversion of vehicle and fuel charges in a more environmentally friendly direction. The strong population growth in the major urban areas over the next 20 years creates exceptional transport needs, as many have mentioned today. In the larger urban areas of Norway, a population growth of 28 pst is expected. from 2010 to 2030. Population growth requires significant capacity expansion in the transport system, regardless of whether it is by car or by more environmentally friendly modes of transport. The forecasts show that around two million more trips will be made daily in the nine largest urban areas in 2030 than in 2010. There is no doubt that one-sided car-based traffic growth will be significantly more expensive for society than a traffic growth managed by public transport and bicycles. A car-based traffic growth will increase climate emissions by 20 pst. If traffic growth occurs in public transport and bicycles, climate emissions will increase marginally. I am therefore very pleased that the climate agreement continues the government's clear objective that the growth of passenger transport, which the population growth of the larger cities brings, should take place through public transport, corridors and bicycles. It's not just what's good for the climate. Less traffic will also provide cleaner air, less noise, increased road safety and, in general, more well-being in cities. However, cities and local authorities already face major challenges in financing the necessary investments and operations in public transport. Last year, the national assembly of the Workers' Party concluded that the State must contribute more to the operation and investment of public transport. The current framework grant to county councils and the reward scheme is not sufficient. The climate agreement follows up this decision by both proposing a strengthening of the reward scheme and stressing the need for the State to contribute more to the operation and investment of public transport in major cities. In Bergen, the city line is now being built from the centre of Flesland to a total price tag of about 7.5 billion. kr. Most of this is paid by the drivers in Bergen. Then the goal is to build the track from the centre to Åsane and Loddefjord respectively. The price estimate from Bergen centrum to Åsane is currently up to NOK 3 billion. In Oslo and Akershus, local politicians agree on a cross-political settlement that ensures that a large proportion of tolls in the next ten years go to public transport. That's good. But to the perhaps most important collective effort in the metropolitan region, a new metropolitan tunnel through Oslo, it is currently only set of 500 million. kr of an investment estimated at 10 billion. kr. This shows a little bit of need. The right sometimes refers to own decisions to prioritize public transport in the four largest cities. But relatively similar economic challenges with investments and the operation of public transport are also found in our second largest cities, such as Tromsø, Kristiansand, Fredrikstad and Drammen. Municipal authorities and county councils should also have primary responsibility for public transport in the future. They know both the challenges and the solutions best locally. On the part of Stortinget, we are going to work for more people to do like Trondheim, where, through the use of public transport and car reduction measures, they have fantastic results with more climate-friendly transport. It is therefore essential that the climate agreement is followed up, so that the State effectively prioritises and secures investments in operation and investment in public transport.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e2755	With this climate announcement, the government has given the forest the place it deserves in Norwegian climate policy. In the climate report, the government states that we are going to use the forest to ensure a high carbon capture of CO2, so that the carbon stock in the forest is constantly increasing. At the same time, we shall use forest resources to produce more renewable energy and wood materials. In Norway the forest covers about 30 pst. of the land area, and the forest takes up each year a CO2 amount corresponding to about half of the total Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions. From 1990 until 2020, the annual net emissions of CO2 in Norwegian forests will increase by 12 million tonnes. Today's high carbon capture in the forest is a result of the work of wise predecessors – the generations before us – who have done through planting and active forestry. At the same time, forecasts show that if we do not implement active measures, the forest will absorb less CO2 in the future. Therefore, the government will follow the advice of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change to maintain active and sustainable forest management. Now we're going to follow up and do our part of the job. In the future, forests will also be one of Norway’s most important contributions to achieving climate change targets. To achieve this, we need to have efforts in a number of areas. The first to be done is to increase the forest area by planting forests in new areas. Climate change has seen estimates of the effect of increasing forest land by 1 million and 5 million deches over 50 years. It will provide an annual additional take-up of 2,2 and 8 million tonnes of CO2 respectively. The Government shall provide a strategy for increasing planting on new areas. The development of ecological criteria will be an important and natural part of this strategy. Then we're going to increase the carbon stock in the forest through an active, sustainable forest policy. An active forest policy is based on several different actions that need to be taken together. In areas where it has been chopped, the densities of the plant shall be increased. The plants we're going to use should be better adapted to today's and tomorrow's climate. That is why we need to strengthen efforts in forest plant processing. Then we will reintroduce the ban on the hoarding of the jungle. The third is that the government will improve the incentives for extracting raw material for bioenergy. Particular attention shall be paid to the extraction of more waste at maximum levels such as branches and peaks, also referred to as GROT. More than half of the measures Climate change investigated, the use of bioenergy involved. The government’s objective of increasing the development of up to 14 TWh bioenergy by 2020 is therefore firm. The Ministry of Agriculture and Food already has an energy tile scheme, which is a good and targeted measure to increase the uptake of forest raw materials into bioenergy. The scheme has been very successful, and the activity has now become so high that the scheme runs out of funds long before the end of the year. We will also investigate a scheme of voluntary climate action in forests, including: the possibility of establishing cooperation agreements with landowners on the establishment of climate forests shall be explored. The idea behind this proposal is to capture a will or desire for more people to contribute to climate action on a voluntary basis. There are already examples of actors — among others: in Nord-Trøndelag County – finances forest carbon capture through forest planting, as ‘payment’ for emissions from an enterprise or emissions related to air travel. Then we will ensure a more conscious choice of climate-friendly construction materials in the construction sector, for example. wood materials which can cause less greenhouse gas emissions in a life-cycle context than other traditional materials. Three-based innovation programmes, funded by the Ministry of Agriculture and Food and managed by Innovation Norway, are an important tool in this respect to increase the competence of good and forward-looking use of wood. Active forest use is a soil-based and concrete climate policy that can be launched from day one, without complex technology. This is also one of the cheapest climate measures we can do. It is also important to take the perspective that good use of forests in climate policy is also good industrial policy.

In the climate communication underlying the agreement, there is a significant impact on a way of thinking about climate policy that SV has been facing for a long time. It is linked to the need for national targets, to the implementation of measures – sector by sector – and to the recognition that we also need action in the sectors covered by quota systems. It's a recognition that we can't help solving climate problems just through out-of-the-world trading. We also need to take action as monks at home in Norway. What also permeates the climate communication is a clear understanding that it is also a contribution to its own future well-being and prosperity. It is by being early in the transition that the world must undergo, that Norway can ensure that we have a competitive business life, that we develop the jobs of the future, and that we create a society that gets better and not worse to live in, for all of us. The low-emission society will be a fantastic society. We need to get away from a debate that draws a picture of the fact that reducing greenhouse gas emissions is primarily about victims and failures. Instead, it's about creating what we're going to live on tomorrow, it's about increased prosperity, it's about better cars and houses, it's about new jobs, new industry, innovation and new technology. Fantastic opportunities for those communities that know how to arrest them. I would like to thank the opposition – three out of four parties – for their constructive contribution to concreteisation on some important points of what has been presented in the Climate Change Communication. It gives us a predictability that this floor will be built on. What we are adopting today is no thanks for climate policy and therefore no end point for the climate debate. Now we need to further challenge each other on the political will and courage needed to achieve what we agreed today, and draw it even further, so that we can achieve the goals.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e3088	SV has claimed for 30 years that good environmental policies can be good industrial policies. There is now a wide-ranging view of both business and policy-makers, and there is a key element in the climate communication. That's SV very happy. In the 1980s, the Norwegian process industry faced major environmental challenges. Industry took the challenges and put technology into action. This led to their strengthening of competitiveness and resulted in their development of an export technology. We would hardly have had a process industry in Norway today if we had not had an offensive environmental policy in the 1980s. So already then a good climate policy – a good environmental policy – strengthened Norwegian industry. Another good example of good environmental policy being good business policy is the NOX. When we got the challenges with NOX, and they got to know, and we were going to do something about it, we were going to bet on a collaboration between the Ministry of the Environment and the industry. This has led us to over-fill the goal of reducing NOX emissions. At the same time, we have strengthened the competitiveness of fishing and shipping. There are, not least, several shipyards in the South and Western countries producing environmental technologies, which are a new market they can enter. It brings me to the fact that environmental technology is a highly expanding market. If you read the UN statement, you will see that environmental technology is one of the strongest growing international markets. When I arrived at Stortinget, one of the things I work hard to get in place, the eco-innovation scheme in Innovation Norway, which is one of the arrangements I get the most credit for when I travel to different companies. The environmental technology system has brought Norwegian industry into a forward-looking market, i.e. the renewable energy market and various forms of clean-up technology. With what is now happening in the climate communication, with the Climate Action Fund, we are raising the focus on environmental technologies, and thus on cooperation between climate and industry, to a new level. We have three positive effects: we have reduced CO2 emissions, we have increased competitiveness for industry, and we have developed a product that allows us to reduce CO2 emissions in other countries, much more than we do at home, because Norway is a high-tech country, which can develop technology that will take a long time to develop in countries with poor technology bases. Hence, we have a cheek egg, CO2 reduction in Norway, strengthened industry and CO2 reduction worldwide.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e3572	The paper on the activities of Post Norway AS and the proposal for changes to the law on the provision of basic banking services are two key issues for an enterprise that is essential for active business activity in the country, so that we can work and live anywhere and ‘use the whole country’. There has been a strong decline in demand for transmission payments over the last few years. At the same time, the use of telephone and Internet banking is increasing. Today, everyone has access to the giro of letters, and cash withdrawals from mini-banks and shops are increasing. These include: on this basis, a unanimous committee advises the Stortinget to limit its obligation to provide basic banking services to the country postal network only. The Committee believes it is important to continue services in the country postal network so that we ensure a good banking offer across the country, regardless of residence, functionality or competence in digital communications. Net withdrawals and payments shall be agreed in advance and the amount of cash held shall be limited to the actual need. This has considerable safety benefits and is labour and cost-saving. Let me link a few comments to the comments of the Party of Advancement and the Right. The Progress Party points out that ten years ago they opposed the Post offering basic banking services, and they believe that the other parties only adopt the Policy of the Advance Party. The right point is that they have made similar proposals in the past. Ten years ago, the situation was quite different. It would have been totally wrong to remove the bank obligation then. It would have great consequences for many. Societies are constantly changing, especially in terms of technology and digitisation, and as politicians, of course, we need to keep up with and deal with this. However, if changes and reorganisations are necessary, the Labour Party believes that this must be done after careful assessments, how many are heard, where the relationships of employees are well cared for, and where account is also taken of those who are unable to deal with the new, digitised world – in other words, everything in their time. We believe that the time has come for this. In the history of the Post, 52 milestones have been found since 1647 and up to today, with large and small conversions. Now, maybe 52 isn't a big number in 365 years, but when we know that it's been 29 since 2000, it says something about an enterprise that doesn't stand still in any way, but that's constantly evolving. It takes me to the next case, the message about the business of Posten Norge AS. The post is currently a Nordic postal and logistics group that develops and delivers comprehensive solutions in postal, communication and logistics. The group has more than 20 000 employees. It has over 1,400 outlets in Norway. In 2011, Posten distributed 38.5 million packages and 2,223 million letters. This shows that the Post every day does an important job for society, which the Committee also stresses the importance of. The Workers' Party has noticed that the Post's vision is to become the world's most forward-looking postal and logistics group, and they have offensive objectives: satisfied customers, leading market positions, profitable growth and competitive value development, attractive jobs and a good working environment. The message addresses many important issues and elements of the Post Office’s business. I'll comment on some of this. In order to carry out the social mission in the best possible way, a unanimous committee calls for equivalent postal services and the rapid and reliable delivery of postal services. The Committee notes unanimously that the unit portfolio should be retained, stressing that sending letters domestically in Norway, irrespective of geographical distances and where the consignment is delivered, should cost the same amount. This is a good comment. I expect that during this debate we will get an explanation of how the Progress Party and the Right will solve this, because it is not easy to understand when you read their further comments and suggestions. In the case of the despatch offer, the customer satisfaction with Post in Store is high. The Committee agrees with the government that the Post may convert up to 149 self-propelled post offices into Post in Store. There shall be at least one permanent dispatch point in each municipality, central location and good availability. If it is ensured that the overall service offer is continued, the Committee believes that the Ministry of Transport can accept a further conversion of the post office to Post in Store, without the matter being referred to the Stortinget. However, if demand or competitive developments change significantly, all conditions shall be presented to the Size. With the decisions we are likely to take today, many hundreds of Post employees will be affected. The Committee assumes that the transitions take place with transparency and in cooperation with the employees and their organisations. The emphasis shall be on a safe and predictable process of change, the aim of which is to get the majority of them to work again. Individual adaptation rooms must be provided and the needs of the perfunctory must be adequately met. The management and staff organisations of the post must cooperate on the conversion measures and paces of the conversions. The Committee has noted that HMS work is a high priority in the group, which has produced good results, including: reduction of sick leave. The Committee commends the Post for further developing good services based on new technologies. Digipost is a good example of this. In the setting of this matter, you can read that the committee is also divided into its comments, and I would like to comment on some of this. The members of the Committee, with the exception of the Progress Party, are very pleased that the Post has continued its social responsibility in relation to the environment and that ambitious objectives have been set for the environmental action, including: to reduce CO2 emissions. These members believe that the Post in this area can be a good example for follow-up. The item has employees from more than 70 nations and is an enterprise of great ethnic diversity. The members of the Committee, with the exception of the Progress Party, are in favour of the Post's objective of increasing the integration and diversity share of the staff and management. There have been a few unfortunate individual pissers where the Post or its subcontractors have been accused of illegal cabotage or have caused problems on Norwegian winter roads. The government parties, the Christian People's Party and the Right have noticed that the Post takes this seriously. The CEO has stated that in the Post and Bring there is zero tolerance for illegal cabotage operations in all parts of the establishment. These members would stress that there should be no illegal cabotage operation in any establishment. It is important that all orders from carrier services, including the Post, do not use carriers that can lead to social dumping and dilution of our work environment legislation. The Right Party and the Advance Party call for more responsible and forward-looking ownership management and believe that the Post does not take on societal change. They refer to the report ‘Delivery obligations in the postal sector’ by Oslo Economics, where estimates show that the size of government purchases of postal services needs to increase to close to 2,5 billion. kr in 2020 to maintain a postal structure identical to that of today. The figure is correct, but what does not emerge is that this amount includes the effect of the Post Office's expected loss of market shares as a consequence of the implementation of the EU's third postal directive. The amount also assumes that no efficiency and cost-saving measures will be implemented during the period. In other words, the calculations have little to do with reality, at least as long as we do not implement the third postal directive. The right hand party and the Progress Party note that the State purchase from the Post Office has been between 300 million. and 500 million. kr in recent years, and that the Item in 2010 repaid part of the aid because of its good annual performance. To that end, the item does not repay part of the appropriation for the purchase of unprofitable postal services because of good annual results. The reason for that payment is that the extra costs of the social mission and the unprofitable portions of the services imposed were lower than estimated. There is again a consequence that the Post, in common with the employees’ organisations, continuously facilitates and implements changes in the production and conversion of staff. efficiencies, e.g.: The Spinnaker project also contributes to reducing the cost of maintaining good postal services in the country. The Right Party and the Progress Party are concerned that the Post must have the freedom to continue its process of change in order to remain competitive, and the Progress Party therefore proposes that the Post should be quoted. The Workers' Party agrees that, in cooperation with its employees, the Post will continue to have the freedom to carry out the necessary change processes but within politically defined frameworks. This space for action has been in use for more than 15 years, with continuous changes and changes. During these years, the number of jobs in the parent company has been reduced by more than 15 000, without a single day of employment conflict. This is really worth noting, so when the Right Party and the Advance Party believe that the Post currently has a small responsibility and forward-looking ownership management, and that the company does not take on societal changes, we simply disagree with it. Right-wing points out that the continuation of today’s postal policy is not sustainable and will therefore set ambitious objectives for the Post Office. One would think that they would then have an active ownership, but they would also have government sales – a little strange, but I find reason to comment on one of the claims. They write, among other things, that the advertising industry is constantly changing and that direct marketing is marginalised. This is not right: the marginalisation of unaddressed C-mail, as the product is actually called, is not part of the image we have seen so far – rather the other way around. Volume has been still growing until now. Nor is the turnover and performance of this product the item expected to be reduced in the coming years. On the right, the item will be sectoral in terms of pay and working conditions. This is mildly alarming, but it is stated in the document, so they must mean it. The return on equity of the company is reduced from 10 to 9 per cent. This is the satisfaction of the Committee, and the Right and Advance Party believe that the requirement should be raised if the company changes its character. The working party agrees that the return claim is being assessed, but I must stress that there must be long-termity and predictability in the decision of the owner, including for the Post Office. In this context, the Right Party and the Advance Party point out that ‘comparable companies’ have had a much higher return than the Post Norway, and refer to Denmark, Germany, PostNord and Itella Group. What is comparable in this context is not one-sided whether there is another postal and/or logistics company. In order to assess whether it really is a comparable company, it is appropriate to have in-depth market knowledge and knowledge of the terms and conditions of the companies, in other words, the concession provisions applicable, if any, and not least to take account of market conditions. It should be clear to the Right and the Advance Party that the distribution costs in Denmark are significantly lower than in Norway, that Sweden, with twice as many people, has twice as many postal items to distribute the determined costs to, and that Germany is very little comparable to Norway. Then go to logistics and ICT. The Committee, with the exception of the Progress Party, has noted that the focus of the group is on profitable growth in the logistics segment and is pleased that the entry in 2011 had growth in turnover in this area. On the other hand, the members of the Committee from the Progress Party recommend an amended strategy with a clearer focus on and concentration in the postal field, and its development and renewal. I find reason to comment that with such a strategy, the company's and the state's values would be confused in a short time. A concentration on the postal sector would, in the short and long term, undermine any possibility for the Post to deliver positive economic results. The alternative will be a severe deterioration in the level of service and quality of Post services and products. In order to provide the services of the quality needed by society, the Post must run a large number of routes each day between different locations: from one terminal to another, from this terminal to a post office or distribution unit and from that unit, for example. to a country postal route. Transport and staff costs are only marginally affected by the number of consignments transported. Instead of driving a half-full car with just letters, you fill the car with parcels and goods. The costs are allocated and revenue increases in relation to having only one focus on the postal sector. In that case, transport costs would be about the same, but they would have to be financed by state aid. Performing the transport and logistics needs as the Posten does is also positive and economical in the external environment. Fewer vehicles mean less CO2 emissions from the largest logistics company in the country. As far as concession requirements and competition are concerned, I have mentioned the unit portfolio. Government parties are committed to ensuring that households and traders in all parts of the country have a good and equivalent postal service, and the level of service must be maintained at the current level. Studies show that 70 pst. the population is satisfied with today's postal distribution, and 50 pst. wish for six-day distribution. Saturday distribution is especially important for customers the older they are, and the more out of focus they live. For us, it is therefore important to maintain the mail delivery six days a week. The Progress Party will also maintain a single portfolio, but at the same time will have a free and open market for postal services, they will have free competition by letter less than 50 grams, and they will implement the third EU Postal Directive. How does this relate? Furthermore, they will cut the service offer and only have the Saturday delivery of newspapers. And the Right also opens for this. In a comment from the Right, they tell us that it is the European Community idea that has been the guarantor of peace and reconciliation and has provided a solid foundation for economic growth in the EEA region. A French quotation from the beginning of the 1800s is highlighted: ‘When goods do not cross borders, soldiers will do so.’ This is used as an argument to make the government not veto the Third Postal Directive. I have to ask: does the Right seriously believe that the government is going to pursue a European policy based on the French policies of the 1800s?
