On the other hand, the members of the Committee from the Right and the Advance Party will end this. The Progress Party will quote the Post and convert it into a so-called public limited liability company, and the Right will facilitate a government sale where small savers and employees will have to purchase shares. In both cases, this shall be done via a so-called public share, where Norwegian citizens who notify their interest receive a discount on their holdings. It may be that strongly discounted shares to the people and the employees had made the move, and that the Post employees had in a way become business owners, each of them. It's possible it would have been good. But the Workers' Party actually believes that the people already own the Post, through the state, and that model we live with. The Progress Party and the Right have a reason why the State's ownership of the Post should not be continued, and this time we are going back to 1647 and the creation of the Post Office. It is referred to that it was Hannibal Sehested, a Danish substitute in Norway, who created the Post Office, and that it was Dutchman Henrik Morian who started the operation. He was granted the exclusive right to operate the Post Office for an annual remuneration. They underline: ‘the business around what is known today as Post Norway had its start as a concession purchase given to a private foreign operator. [[...]] There is therefore no news that private actors are contributing to a task for which the State has overall responsibility.» So this is going to write the progress part and the right part of the setting. I cannot understand this in any other way than that the Right Party and the Advance Party agree that private monopolies, like foreign ones, are top, while state monopolies are not good – really no surprise. The right also justifies its proposal on State sales with the fact that enterprises in the postal sector are by nature international. I also have a comment on this: the international nature of the industry is that the postal items are carried across borders, from one consignor in one country to one receiver in another. Until a few years ago, this occurred without exception by the fact that, in sum, the national postal companies constituted the world web, bringing the international post to the national company, which was responsible for the further distribution together with the national postal services in the respective countries. In the liberalised market, each company itself must build this entire network to participate in international competition. How can one believe and believe that it provides better and more reasonable postal services if two or more postal cars and postal couriers drive and walk side by side or in succession and deliver the mail to the same mailbox? The experience of the countries that have liberalised the postal market is quite the opposite. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e3799	The post has to go. The overriding objective of the government in the postal field is to ensure the provision of universal postal services throughout the country. Postal services are important to sustain construction and business in all parts of our long-range country. I am pleased that the Committee is joining this goal of the Post. The ownership of Posten Norway AS is an important sector policy tool for government. Already when the Norwegian Post Office was created in 1647, mail distribution was considered an important service to ensure good communication. Society's demands for good services have changed over the years, and the Post Office became a company on 1 December 1996. In the years that followed, the post has also made major adjustments to adapt to a new time. Digital communication has exceeded much of the communication previously made by the Post. At the same time, the Post has provided high-quality universal services and solved the challenges in a forward-looking way. People today are happy with the service they are provided in Post in Store. This was not the case when the first post offices were converted in 2001. Now measurements show that customers are most satisfied with Post in Store as with Post's own post office. Even if there are long distances and the winter in Norway is weather-hard, come even over 85 pst. by post-A over the night, measured on an annual basis. Of course, there are periods when this does not happen, but the Post is good at finding good options for the mail. That's what we saw during the ash crisis, and we see it in other special events. If the Post delivers good quality services and delivers good economic results, there are still challenges in the future. Letters are falling, competition in the packaging market is increasing, and Post is experiencing increased competition from digital messaging channels. The mail needs to be converted to fit the new time and the service supply to the request. That is why we in the message and in the bill have presented three changes that are very important in adapting the Post's activity to the demand for services: The obligation to provide banking services is reduced to the country postal network only. The post office will be sent to Post in Store until 149. The post may be created as PiB Simple, a dispatch point where only the most requested services will be provided. The demand for banking services in the post offices has fallen sharply in recent years. A lot of this happens because people are increasingly using competing services like online banking, telephone banking, and letter giro. Containers can take out into mini-banks and shops around the country. At the same time, there has been an offer to pay bills in the so-called Bank in Butikk. Some of the places with Post in Store are also Bank in Store, so customers can get the same services in two boxes in the same store. Nevertheless, there are people in Norway who do not experience the same freedom of choice as most of us do. In some districts it is far to the nearest banking branch or bank in Storeik, and some do not use the Internet. It has therefore been important for me to ensure that everyone has a bank offer. Copenhagen Economics has identified that if the bank supply to the Post disappears, there are about 12,000 people in pig-grown strips who get more than four kilometres to the nearest banking destination. I understand that not everyone in this room cares about that very much, but in the bill we have stated that the Post Office's obligation to provide banking services is passed on in the country postal network. The post operates today 179 post offices. When the demand for mail and banking services goes down, there are still fixed costs associated with running these offices. A transformation into Post in Store will reduce determined costs and improve revenue and costs in relation to each other. Furthermore, transformation from post office to Post in Store will often give longer opening hours. The message also sets requirements for Mail in Stores to be added to locations with good access from existing collective services. I am also pleased that the Committee agrees that a further conversion of post offices does not need to be dealt with in the Stortinget but can be dealt with by the Ministry. Conversion frames added at post offices. The post office that is being created is not set. I am sure that the Post will have a good process associated with the conversion that needs to be done. The message requires that they be added to the process and that as much as possible be made possible to prevent the superstitious from becoming unemployed or ending up in a social security system. The government attaches great importance to improving the quality of mail services. As a result, there are requirements in the message that the number of fully-validated dispatch locations, i.e. post office and post office in Store, shall be at least 1420. The post office wants to create so-called PiB Simple, dispatch locations that only offer some services, such as package delivery. In addition to the places where one provides all universal services, these places of dispatch will improve people’s access to postal services. The post also aims at providing services for secure electronic messaging, Digipost. I mean it so absolutely is a good idea that supports up under the Post's core business. I hope this is an approach that will ensure good profitability and good quality in the postal services at a time when the Post is facing major challenges. At the same time, it is important to hold on to the Post's obligation to deliver mail on Saturdays, that universal services should cost the same in the whole country, and that the Post should have a single right to receive a letter of less than 50 grams. The entry is one of Norway's largest corporations. I have let myself be impressed by the Post's environmental work. I myself have been sharing packages in a zero-emission car in Trondheim. It's part of the Post's goal of being a leader in logistics and thinking new when it comes to the environment. I'm glad that the attempt they've made there will also be passed on to other areas. Then I must say that I am a little surprised that not the whole committee felt that the integration work that the Post is dealing with is positive. Not everyone could gather around, blatantly integrating, and there are, in fact, 70 nationalities that are represented as employees in the Post. It's positive with the extra amount of deficiency. The SV parties, the Workers' Party, the Centre Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party support the government's proposal that the project be financed by state grants and tolls, while the Progress Party proposes that the whole process be financed by full government funding. It seems to me that this is a piece of work between the state and the drivers. It is then worth noting that this case fully shows how hopelessly expensive it is to build roads on payment. The starting point for this distance is that it will cost 420 million. kr to build up the road, and the proposition states that the State will grant 200 million. kr, and that the drivers have to pay 200 million. kr. But in addition to this, the drivers have to pay approx. $180 million. NOK in interest expenses and SEK 45 million. NOK to collect the money over a 15-year period. That means that the drivers actually have to pay $445 million. NOK to pay a road costing 420 million. to build. So this means a project of 420 million. The cost to society is 645 million. kr. It refers to the total madness this way of financing road construction is. With this way of financing road construction, it is no wonder that it takes a long time to build up the road network in Norway. When you look at the expansion of the road width as the proposition suggests that the road should be expanded, it also fully shows that today's government has not learned anything from the faults they themselves, with the State Council of Meltveit Kleppa in the head, many times our predecessors have criticized in this room: 40 years ago they built so narrow roads that that's why we can't expand more midway faster. With this as the back carpet, I had really hoped that the government would have come up with better thoughts, but in the proposition, one would add up to a road width of 8.5 meters. This means that if it turns out that there are going to be accidents on the new road, you don't have the opportunity to set up, among other things. middle parts, because the road is too narrow. It should be a prerequisite that, when new national roads are built, they must be built with a standard that ensures the possibility of road safety measures and that the road is dimensioned for the future. For no one knows what it will cost to do this once in the future, but everyone knows that it's going to be swine. It seems to me that when you build such projects, you look at first: How much can the drivers get tolled? And then you look at how much money you're willing to deposit from the state, and then you build the way out of it. Instead, what standard should we have on a forward-looking road? And then you build a way like that. I had hoped that the council and the government had learned from their predecessors 40 years ago, but unfortunately we will probably see that this is not the case today. But for both me and the Advancement Party, it is a joyous day every time we in this room make the decision to roll out the asphalt in a large moon and build out and repair our roads. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e4503	Today, the size of the population deals with two issues which have the common aim of facilitating more effective cross-border criminal prosecution. Firstly, a legislative proposal will be considered to facilitate the conclusion by Norway of two international agreements on mutual assistance in criminal matters, one with the EU and a European Council agreement. Secondly, the question of the conclusion of those agreements shall be dealt with. Unfortunately, as the rapporteur has explained, there was an error in the bill, and I am grateful that the committee is correcting it. It's an accident at work of the kind that's not going to happen. These instruments have already been practised in the EU and the Council of Europe for several years and are working well. The agreements are based on and complement the conventions and agreements already concluded by Norway, including: The European Convention on Mutual Assistance in Criminal Matters and the Schengen Convention. The aim of the agreements is to promote closer international cooperation in the fight against crime. Legal cooperation is made simpler and more flexible, for example through simplified rules for the submission of legal applications. The agreements further regulate some forms of legal cooperation that are not previously Conventional, such as: hearings at video and telephone conferences and police cooperation in the form of inter alia: joint investigation team and secret investigation. The EU Convention also has rules on information on electronic communications and information relating to banking transactions. The agreements do not, in my opinion, raise specific questions and have only prompted one comment from the Judicial Committee. I am, of course, pleased that the Storting gives its consent to ratification. The Schengen area of freedom of movement means that crime is also increasingly cross-border. There is therefore a growing need to remove legal and factual obstacles to cross-border investigation and prosecution. International judicial cooperation in the field of criminal law is necessary and it is important that as many countries as possible participate in such cooperation, so that no country is tempted to commit crimes. I am concerned that Norway must also monitor developments in this area, particularly within Europe, and should join new international agreements on criminal law cooperation.

If you believe that this proposal has been promoted in order to ask for changes in the law, that is not true. So you lose the ability to cross mistakes, you just make it easier. In fact, I would like to pay tribute to the Minister of Finance, to the Minister of Agriculture, and also to the Red-Green parties on the Stortinget, because we have had a positive development. ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e5068	In this case, there is broad consensus in the committee, and the vote is unanimous in its entirety. That's good. The Committee endorses the intention of the proposal by Hanne Thürmer, Laila Davøy and Kjell Ingolf Ropstad and supports the inclusion of the protocol. We are all deeply concerned about the development of ever-new synthetic drugs. I think the president otherwise pronounced them elegant, so I'm just saying synthetic drugs that give users – essentially young people – a high health risk. The substances are speculatively manufactured to circumvent the law, and the only purpose is to give drugs. As soon as possible, we need to put in place a law to ensure effective and rapid inclusion in the drug list when such new substances appear. Today it often takes too much time, and so it should not continue. In a letter from Minister of Health and Health Anne-Grete Ström-Erichsen to the Committee, the Minister explained that in the summer the State Medicines Agency will present a new draft drug regulation, including a drug definition. This has been discussed in the past, but in order to improve legislation, and in the light of the input made in that consultation round, there is a new draft that will soon be available. Then follow an ordinary hearing. The Ministry has asked the Swedish Medical Products Agency to look at the recommendations made by the United Nations professional body in this area, i.e. an assessment of whether to have a generic definition of this in order to cover as many types of substances as possible. This means that a new regulatory framework, such as: shall contribute to the elimination of interpretability, may be in place within a reasonable time. We need to have high ambitions that new legislation should make a significant difference in the fight against the drug industry and in order to better prevent drug abuse. The Committee has chosen to refer to the views of the police lawyers in its capacity. Moreover, police lawyers are generally quite active and constructive towards the Committee. As rapporteur of the case, I have had a dialogue with the police lawyers. They want a bag order – as they themselves call it in a letter I've been given – that captures as many new drugs as possible. Here it is that one in this area thinks quite equally in the department compared to what the police lawyers do of assessments. We also refer to Rusfeld’s cooperation body, Actis, which points out that Sweden has a law that allows authorities to destroy substances with harm and potential for abuse in the case of substances that are not yet on the drug list. From the Committee's point of view, it may be interesting to consider whether this might be relevant for Norway. I believe that the proposal of the three Christian People's Party representatives is well taken care of by this unanimous vote, if, on the basis of the current regulatory process, we do not support the proposal itself. I hope that the Christian People's Party – and the Left, which is not on the committee either – can join in this way of dealing with it. It's important that the process goes fast. Good quality assurance of the work now being provided by the Agency and the Ministry is also very important. It is, of course, through regulatory work that one here gets the legislation in place to bring us new steps forward in the fight against the drug industry. Today, the size sends important and clear signals that we will go more strongly to work — using stronger lye — to combat this cynical activity by the drug industry, which affects so many in our society and which we really need to take seriously. We show the seriousness when we stand together, as we do today. So thank the members of the committee for a very constructive cooperation around this setting.

One thing is the health challenges: I'm happy in zombie movies, but in the so-called zombie attacks that have been in the United States, you're wondering if it can be caused by what's been called 'badesalt' that you've sold in the shops. This is a form of legal means that people use, which can lead to them becoming extremely aggressive, getting hallucinations and committing gross crime. So this is an incredibly important proposal that we now agree to attach to the protocol, but which hopefully will be followed up properly by the government. In conclusion, I would like to say that it is important to look at what is sold as a ‘health food’ in Norwegian shops in parallel with this work. Here, there has been a narrowing, but we know that in the past, there has been a discovery that there are preparations and resources sold over the counter – and not just under the counter – in Norwegian shops today, which can contain quite a lot of the substances that can create both the one and the other reactions of those who use it. I've tried a drug myself. It was too many, many years ago in Spain. It was very nice to use for training and bought at a very good health care store, but I discovered very quickly that – in addition to almost not getting a pulse – I became very angry, did not sleep and became very aggressive. There was certainly no marked difference, but at least there was no particularly pleasant experience, and I ended up with it after quite a short time. But after looking at some websites, I think there are actually shops in Norway that still sell that kind of money. So it is important to work closely with the EU and international organisations to keep up-to-date on what the industry is doing. For some, not only do they sell illegal funds; they combine where they can collect money and where they find loopholes in the legislation, they sell both over the network and under the counter. So when someone wants to strengthen the Tolls' possibilities, this is a piece of it next to what I picked up now. So I want to thank the proposers again for a good proposal, and I hope that we will get a case for the Storting relatively quickly, so that we can make some decisions and implement what we all agree on.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-06-11.ana.ud535e5142	As many people before me, I would like to take the opportunity to thank the proposers for putting this important theme on the agenda – even if you can almost call it a night order. Getting legislation to ensure that these synthetic cannabinoids and synthetic opioids come under drug law will be an important measure to combat the increasing use of these substances and thus all the adverse effects they cause. A short time ago, I attended a conference directed by Sirus. The main theme was ‘What do we know about cannabis?’ The initiators considered this to be a result of several factors, including: that most people have gained more knowledge about the serious health damage caused by cannabis. However, we see a dramatic increase in the use of synthetic cannabinoids. The sale has completely gone off the Internet. These drugs, also marketed as ‘Legal Highs’, were sold on a total of 693 websites in January this year, compared with 314 in January last year, i.e. more than one year’s doubling. More and more types of substances are emerging on the market. The market is inconclusive and the content of these products is often a well-kept secret. It's scary how little we really know about what kind of substances are in the different products, and not least the effects of these substances. What we know, however, is that the drug can cause overdoses because the effect increases proportionally with the intake, as opposed to, for example, herbal cannabis. Serious adverse reactions such as myocardial infarction have been reported at 17 years of age. Psychosis with self-injury, accidents and deaths has also been reported. When we know how dangerous these substances are, it's scary to hear how innocent the young people who order this online think they are. Users are most concerned with the benefits of use rather than the adverse effects. The benefits of the list of young people are everything from cheap, easy access, regularity, guarantee of delivery, strong – in fact, two to three hundred times more than usual hashish – drug withdrawals quickly, and not being detected in urine samples. If we do not have anything to do with the attitudes to the use of these substances, I am afraid that in the future we will see more users, younger users, an increased need for treatment and an increased use of other drugs. This kind of development we have to do everything we can to prevent. A law that allows these synthetic drugs to come under drug law is, as I said, important, both because it gives a signal effect and because, among other things, it does. make police work to combat both sales, purchases and use much easier. But such a change of laws will not be enough alone. In addition, we need to know more about the substances and the adverse health and social impacts. Furthermore, we need to be much better off getting this important knowledge out — out to young people, out to parents and out to people most to prevent people from being tempted to take these drugs. The work must therefore be intensified. My opinion is that only then will we have the opportunity to succeed in combating the use of these synthetic cannabinoids.
